Nonlocal Meyers’ Example Thanks: Anna Kh. Balci is funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) - SFB 1283/2 2021 - 317210226, Charles University in Prague PRIMUS/24/SCI/020 and Research Centre program No. UNCE/24/SCI/005. Lars Diening and Moritz Kassmann gratefully acknowledge funding by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) - SFB 1283/2 2021 - 317210226. Anna Kh. Balci, Lars Diening, Moritz Kassmann, Ho-Sik Lee

Abstract
. We present nonlocal variants of the famous Meyers’ example of limited higher integrability and differentiability. In the limit s ↗ 1 s\nearrow 1 we recover the standard Meyers’ example. We consider the fractional Laplacian based on differences as well as the one based on fractional derivatives defined by Riesz potentials.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary:
35R09, 35B65; Secondary:
35A15, 35D30, 47G20.

1.
Introduction

Critical regularity results for partial differential equations have been studied for a long time and have led to fruitful research. A regularity result stating an additional regularity property of solutions to some equation under a certain condition on the data (coefficients, right-hand side, boundary of domain) is called
optimal
or
sharp
if the solutions do not possess this additional property in general if the aforementioned condition on the data fails. Usually, it is rather challenging to produce counterexamples proving that additional regularity is limited in general. It is the purpose of this paper to open up this analysis for integrodifferential operators of fractional order
2s\in(0,2)
and to address the limits of self-improving properties for such nonlocal operators.

Let us explain self-improving properties in the fractional case provided in
[
BR13
,
KMS15
,
Sch16
,
ABES19
]
. Given a coefficient function
a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
, which is symmetric and satisfies
\lambda\leq a(x,y)\leq\Lambda
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the self-improving property means that a weak solution
u\in W^{s,2}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(\Omega)
(with a certain tail condition) to

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}u(x)=\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{u(x)-u(y)}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,a(x,y)\,dy=0

in an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
u\in W^{s+\varepsilon,2+\varepsilon}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(\Omega)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. One very challenging question is to find upper bounds for
\varepsilon
, which are sharp with regard to assumptions on the coefficient function
a(x,y)
. Our main result contributes to this question by providing examples of coefficient functions
a(x,y)
together with explicit solutions to the equation allowing for sharp regularity statements. One of our results is the following:

Theorem
[see Theorem
4.1
].
Let
d\geq 2
and
s\in(0,1)
. For every
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
there is a coefficient function
a_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
, and
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
such that for
u_{\delta}(x):={\lvert x\rvert}^{-\delta}x_{1}
we have
u_{\delta}\in W^{s,2}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}u_{\delta}=0
in
B_{1}(0)
with
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
being defined as in (
1.1
) replacing
a(x,y)
with
a_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
. Furthermore, the following properties hold:

(a)

The range and the oscillation of the coefficient functions
a_{\varepsilon}
decrease as
\varepsilon
decreases, with
a_{0}(x,y)\equiv 1
.

(b)

The map
\varepsilon\mapsto\delta
defined above is strictly increasing with
\varepsilon\leq\overline{c}(d,s)\delta
and
\delta(0^{+})=0
.

(c)

For all
t>0
and
q\in(1,\infty)
, we have

(1.2)

\displaystyle u_{\delta}\in W_{{\mathrm{loc}}}^{t,q}\,\,\text{for}\,\,1-\delta>t-\frac{d}{q}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\delta}\not\in W_{{\mathrm{loc}}}^{t,q}\,\,\text{for}\,\,1-\delta\leq t-\frac{d}{q}.

Note that the function
u_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is modeled after
[
Mey63
,
BDGN22
]
. Theorem
4.1
contains several more details, in particular on the regularity of the coefficient function
a_{\varepsilon}
. Let us collect some important remarks.

(i)

A detailed discussion of property (a) in the above theorem is important because solutions are known to be more regular if the coefficient
a_{\varepsilon}
satisfies additional assumptions (see
[
KNS22
,
DMN25
]
). The significance of the above result is that it indicates that sharp regularity results are possible even for nonlocal operators of fractional order. Of course, it has direct consequences, too. For example, (
1.2
) says that solutions, in general, are not Lipschitz-continuous in the interior of
\Omega
, since
\delta>0
.

(ii)

The coefficient functions
a_{\varepsilon}
that we work with are defined by

a_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}{\big\langle{\left({\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(x)+{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(y)\right)(x-y)},{x-y}\big\rangle}\,,

with an appropriately chosen matrix-valued function
{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}
. In Theorem
4.1
we provide a link between the regularity of
{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}
and the regularity of
u_{\delta}
. To be precise, there is a number
c(d,s)>1
such that for every
t\in(0,1)
and
q\in[2,\infty)
there is a uniformly elliptic matrix-valued function
{\mathbb{M}}
satisfying
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{M}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}\leq c(d,s)(1-t+\frac{d}{q})
and a solution
u
to the equation
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
B_{1}(0)
with coefficients
a(x,y)
defined by
{\mathbb{M}}
with
u\notin W_{{\mathrm{loc}}}^{t,q}
. This observation is analogous to the one made in
[
BDGN22
]
for linear partial differential equation of the form
-\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{M}}^{2}\nabla u)=0
. Given
q\in(2,\infty)
, the assumption
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{M}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{c}{q}
implies
\nabla u\in L^{q}
. The corresponding Meyers-type counterexample yields that the linear dependence of this implication with respect to
q
is optimal.

(iii)

Theorem
4.1
is formulated for a fixed choice of
s\in(0,1)
. However, we point out that the results in this paper are robust as
s\nearrow 1
in several ways. On the one hand we show in Lemma
2.3
that the corresponding energies converge to a local gradient-type energy for a given function
u
as
s\nearrow 1
. In Theorem
4.2
we also study the convergence of the operators applied to
u_{\delta}
.

(iv)

The regularity of
u_{\delta}
is the worst for
d=2
in the sense that the additional regularity of
u_{\delta}
compared to a
W^{s,2}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}
function becomes greater for larger
d
. This is in accordance with
[
Mey63
]
, where the case
d\geq 3
is constructed by the method of descent: the two dimensional example is extended constantly in the additional variables.

1.1.
Regularity results for nonlocal equations

In the recent decades, the study of the regularity theory for linear nonlocal problems involving operators of the form (
1.1
), which are versions of the fractional Laplace operator

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{u(x)-u(y)}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dy\quad(d\in\mathbb{N},\,\,s\in(0,1))\,,

has evolved in several directions. Early contributions concerning Hölder regularity and Harnack inequality include the works
[
BL02
,
CK03
,
Sil06
,
Kas09
]
. Imposing some regularity assumptions on the coefficients, interior higher order regularity
is proved in
[
CS09
,
CS11
,
CD12
,
KL12
,
Kri13
,
KL21
]
.

Calderón-Zygmund type estimates are proved in
[
MaY21
]
assuming that, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
a(x,\cdot)
is Hölder continuous, see also
[
FMSY22
]
. Nonlocal self-improving properties were shown in
[
BR13
,
KMS15
,
Sch16
,
ABES19
]
.
[
Now21
]
studies regularity properties of nonlocal equations with continuous coefficients and shows higher Hölder regularity.
[
Now23a
,
Now23
]
show higher Sobolev-regularity by assuming a VMO-condition for
a(\cdot,\cdot)
. Potential and gradient potential estimates are proved in
[
KPU11
,
KMS15
,
NNSW23
]
and
[
KNS22
,
DKLN24
]
, respectively. We do not comment on interesting regularity results for recently studied nonlinear problems including the fractional
p
-Laplace equation. For the Hölder continuity of solutions to problems with degenerate coefficients, we refer to
[
BDOR24
]
.

Let us mention that another possibility to formulate nonlocal equations involving operators of differentiability order
2s
is given by

(1.3)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{div}^{s}(\mathbb{A}(x)\nabla^{s}u)=0,

where
\mathbb{A}(x)
is a uniformly elliptic matrix,
\operatorname{div}^{s}
and
\nabla^{s}
are defined with the help of the Riesz potential, see
[
SS15
]
. In Section
5
we provide a Meyers-type example for equations of the form (
1.3
), too. Again we show limited regularity of the solution in terms of
s,d
, and
\varepsilon
, similar to the theorem above.

For some of our computations it will be very important that the fractional Laplace operator
(-\Delta)^{s}
can represented as a simple multiplication operator when the Fourier transform is applied. To be precise, for every Schwartz function
v
and every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[(-\Delta)^{s}v](\xi)=(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2s}\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)

The study of fractional operators with the help of the Fourier transform and harmonic analysis has been intensively carried out for very long. We refer the reader to the references in
[
Jac01
]
and
[
Gru15
]
. Note that fractional operators for vector-valued functions such as for fractional harmonic maps or O’hara knot energies have also been considered
[
DR11
,
Sch12
,
BRS16
]
.

One interesting question appearing in the study of operators of fractional order
2s\in(0,2)
is, whether the results are robust as
s\nearrow 1
. Here, robustness means that the constants in the estimates under consideration remain bounded when
s
converges to
1
. This question is closely related to results like
[
BBM02
]
and the study of function spaces of fractional order. Note that several of the aforementioned results are robust whereas other results are not. Let us mention one example. As explained above, weak solutions
u\in W^{s,2}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(\Omega)
to (
1.1
) in an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
u\in W^{s+\varepsilon,2+\varepsilon}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(\Omega)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. The corresponding estimate is not
s
-robust and it is incorrect if
s=1
.

1.2.
Higher integrability for elliptic local equations

The concept of weak solutions includes integrability of the gradient of the solutions. Let us list a few classical results proving higher integrability in the local case. To this end, let
{\mathbb{A}}(x)
denote for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
a symmetric matrix, which is uniformly positive definite in
x
. As shown in
[
Boy57
]
for the two-dimensional case, weak solutions
u
to the equation
-\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{A}}(x)\nabla u)=0
satisfy higher integrability for
|\nabla u|
. The case of arbitrary dimensions and a class of counterexamples is covered in
[
Mey63
,
BDGN22
]
. Note that these counterexamples are the main inspiration of the current article. Let us also mention that additional assumptions on the coefficients lead to higher regularity.
[
Di 96
]
assumes that the mean oscillation of
{\mathbb{A}}
vanishes, whereas
[
CP98
]
assumes some Cordes-type condition and
[
BW04
]
assumes that the the BMO-norm of
{\mathbb{A}}
is small. Analogous regularity results have been proved also or solutions to degenerate elliptic equations with Muckenhoupt weights, see
[
FKS82
,
CMP18
,
BDGN22
]
. Note that the results in
[
BDGN22
]
show optimal higher integrability exponents measured in terms of BMO norm of
\log{\mathbb{A}}
, thus complementing Meyers’ example. Similar results relating the boundary regularity and the optimal higher integrability have been proved in
[
BBDL23
]
. We also like to mention the interesting results of
[
Tru71
,
BS21
]
where different conditions on the coefficients are assumed.

1.3.
Meyers’ example in the local case

Let us begin with a review of this classical example based on
[
Mey63
]
. The details for all statements below can be found in the recent presentation in
[
BDGN22
, Example 24]
. Let
B_{1}(0)\subset{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
denote the unit ball of
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
d\geq 2
, and write
\widehat{x}_{1}=\frac{x_{1}}{{\lvert x\rvert}}
and
\widehat{x}=\frac{x}{{\lvert x\rvert}}
. With
\delta,\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we define
u_{\delta}\,:\,B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}
and the matrix-valued coefficient
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}\,:\,B_{1}(0)\to{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{{\mathrm{sym}}}}
by

(1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} u_{\delta}(x)&\coloneqq{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1},\\
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)&\coloneqq(1-\varepsilon)\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}.\end{aligned}

Then for any
x\in B_{1}(0)
and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}|\xi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\varepsilon)|\xi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)\xi},{\xi}\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq|\xi|^{2}

holds and so
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly elliptic. Moreover,
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon
, where
\log
is the matrix logarithm and
{\lVert\cdot\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}
is the BMO-seminorm on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. If we impose

(1.6)

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\frac{d-\delta}{d-1}\delta,

then
u_{\delta}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
satisfies

(1.7)

\displaystyle(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}})u_{\delta}(x)=-\operatorname{div}\big({\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)\nabla u_{\delta}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{on $B_{1}(0)$}

in the weak sense. Although
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly elliptic, the integrability of
\nabla u_{\delta}
is limited. In fact,
\nabla u_{\delta}
only belongs to the Marcinkiewicz space
L^{\frac{d}{\delta},\infty}(B_{1}(0))
. As a consequence
u_{\delta}\in W^{1,\rho}(B_{1}(0))
if and only if
\rho<\frac{d}{\delta}
.

This example was used in
[
Mey63
]
to show that there is no minimal gain of higher integrability of
\nabla u
if the condition number of the coefficient
\mathbb{A}(\cdot)
goes to infinity, where
u
is a solution of
-\operatorname{div}\big({\mathbb{A}}(x)\nabla u(x)\big)=0
. In
[
BDGN22
, Theorem 1]
it has been shown more explicitly that the smallness of
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{A}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}
is directly linked to the exponent of integrability of
\nabla u
. In fact,
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{A}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon
implies
\nabla u\in L^{q}(B_{1}(0))
for all
q\in[2,\infty)
with
q\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon}
for some
\kappa=\kappa(d)
. The example of Meyers’ shows that this linear dependence of
{\lVert\log{\mathbb{A}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}}
and
\frac{1}{q}
is sharp (up to the choice of
\kappa
).

1.4.
Strategy in the nonlocal setting

Let us explain the main challenges in the proof of our main result together with ideas how to approach them. We concentrate on three main issues: computation of the appropriate coefficients
a(x,y)
in the operator given in (
1.1
), adjusting the energy to the growth of the function
u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{-\delta}x_{1}
at infinity and how to apply the operator under consideration to
u_{\delta}
.

Computation of the coefficients
a(x,y)
in the operator
\mathcal{L}
from (
1.1
)
. One challenge is to incorporate the matrix function
\mathbb{A}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
from (
1.5
) into the fractional operator
\mathcal{L}
from
(
1.1
)
. One option is to apply the Fourier transform. For a moment, consider a given positive definite symmetric constant matrix
\mathbb{A}
and
{\mathbb{M}}^{2}=\mathbb{A}
and define an operator
-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}
by
-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}v\coloneqq\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{A}}\nabla v)=\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{M}}^{2}\nabla v)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}v\big]=\left(2\pi{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}\xi\rvert}\right)^{2}\mathcal{F}[v].

Inspired by this, one could define
\left(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{s}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[\left(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{s}v\big]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(2\pi{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}\xi\rvert}\right)^{2s}\mathcal{F}[v]\,,

or, equivalently, for a sufficiently regular function
v

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{s}v(x)

\displaystyle=-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{v(x+{\mathbb{M}}h)-v(x)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\quad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{d})

with some constant
\kappa_{d,s}
depending on
d
and
s
. This would justify to work with the non-translation invariant versions of the form

\displaystyle\left(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)}\right)^{s}v(x)

\displaystyle=-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{v(x+{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}(x)h)-v(x)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh,

with
\mathbb{A}_{\varepsilon}
as in (
1.5
) and
\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{A}_{\varepsilon}
. The corresponding nonlocal energy would be

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}_{{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{{\lvert v(x+{\mathbb{M}}_{\varepsilon}(x)h)-v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\,dx.

However, to verify that a certain function is a minimizer of this energy seems an impossible task and thus we choose another approach. We apply Taylor approximation with respect to
\varepsilon
, see Section
2.5
, and arrive at the following more accessible energy:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)\coloneqq\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}}{|y-x|^{d+2s}}a_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\,dy\,dx\,,

where the coefficient function
a_{\varepsilon}(x,y):\mathbb{R}^{2d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(1.8)

\displaystyle a_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\coloneqq{\Big\langle{\left(\tfrac{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)}{2}\right)\widehat{x-y}},{\widehat{x-y}}\Big\rangle}\,,

and

(1.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)\coloneqq\left(1-\tfrac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\right)\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x},

where
\hat{x}
and
\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}
is defined in Subsection
1.3
. Let us point out some nice properties of the quadratic form
v\mapsto\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)
:

•

The energy
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)
converges to
\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}\nabla v},{\nabla v}\rangle}
as
s\nearrow 1
, see Lemma
2.3
.

•

The corresponding nonlocal operator applied to the function
u_{\delta}
satisfies scaling and rotation properties, see Remark
4.5
.

•

The action of the nonlocal operator can be explicitly computed, see Section
4
, the Appendix
A
and the auxiliary computations in Appendix
B
.

How to adjust the energy to the growth of the function
u_{\delta}
at infinity
. Similarly to
[
Mey63
]
, we use the function
u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}
for some
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
as a solution to our problem. However,
\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(u_{\delta})
with
u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}
is infinite because of the growth of
u_{\delta}(x)
at infinity. Analogously to
[
CMY17
,
DPV19
]
, we renormalize the energy
\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}^{s}(v)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{s}(v)\coloneqq\dfrac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\dfrac{\big({\lvert v(y)-v(x)\rvert}^{2}-{\lvert u_{\delta}(y)-u_{\delta}(x)\rvert}^{2}\big)}{{\lvert y-x\rvert}^{d+2s}}a_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\,dy\,dx.

Additionally for
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we need to interpret the energy by a principal value integral, see Section
2.7
. Furthermore, by Proposition
2.6
we guarantee that there exists a unique minimizer and we can consider the Euler-Lagrange equation. Moreover, due to Lemma
2.5
the object
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u
, which is originally defined in the sense of distributions, turns out to be a locally integrable function, see Lemma
3.1
. The technical difficulties with the growth at infinity are the main reason that we have to restrict the range of
\delta
to
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

How to evaluate the operator under consideration applied to
u_{\delta}
. Our main result Theorem
4.1
shows that both, the higher integrability and differentiability of the solution of the nonlocal fractional equation are limited in a quantitative way by the condition on the coefficients. The analogous result in the non-fractional case is given in
[
BDGN22
, Example 24]
. We would like to point out that there are only very few examples involving explicit computations for the equation involving fractional Laplacian, e.g., in
[
Dyd12
,
Gru24
]
. The presence of the integral in the nonlocal equation makes the computation difficult.

We approach these difficulties by finding a convolution structure of our equation after making use of scaling and rotation properties. This enables us to work with the Fourier transform. Overall, computing
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)
is reduced to calculating

\displaystyle(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})=-\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\Big[\left(1-\tfrac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\right)\tfrac{2}{\kappa_{d,s}}(-\Delta)^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})+\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon f_{2}(d,s,\delta)\Big],

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f_{2}(d,s,\delta)=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\dfrac{{\langle{e_{1}+h},{h}\rangle}^{2}}{|e_{1}+h|^{2}|h|^{2}}+\dfrac{{\langle{e_{1}},{h}\rangle}^{2}}{|h|^{2}}\bigg)\dfrac{|e_{1}+h|^{-\delta}(1+h_{1})-1}{|h|^{d+2s}}\,dh\,.\end{split}

See (
B.2
) and (
4.13
) below for
f_{2}(d,s,\delta)
. The last expression may look complicated but it contains a convolution structure thus it can be computed precisely via the Fourier transform, see Appendix
A
.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

Section
2
is devoted to introducing the nonlocal energy based on the Fourier transform, and to derive its Euler-Lagrange equation. In Section
3
we prove that our operator
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}
applied to
u_{\delta}
is actually a locally integrable function. In Section
4
we provide the nonlocal Meyers’ example. Adjusting
\varepsilon
and
\delta
will give
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u=0
. Section
5
contains an example for (
1.3
) using Riesz fractional derivatives. In Appendix
A
we precisely compute the term
f_{2}
from above with the help of the Fourier transform. In Appendix
B
we provide justifications of convolutions and convolution theorems which are necessary for the proof.

2.
Nonlocal equations with coefficients

Different from the local case it is a priori not obvious, what an equation such as (
1.7
) should look like in the nonlocal case. In this section we derive nonlocal models with coefficients that are inspired by the idea that one should recover (
1.7
) in the limit
s\nearrow 1
.

2.1.
Standard fractional Laplacian

Let us briefly recall the definition of the fractional Laplacian and introduce some notation.

Let
d\geq 2
denote the dimension. By
B_{r}(x)
we denote the open ball of radius
r
with center
x
. By
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
we denote the standard space of Schwartz functions. For
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let us use the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}u(x)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\,dx.

The Fourier transform is extended to the space of tempered distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
.

The Fourier symbol of
-\Delta u
is
(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2}
. This allows us to define the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}u
for
s\in(0,1)
by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[(-\Delta)^{s}u\big](\xi)=(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2s}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi).

The fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
also can be defined equivalently in terms of differences of functions. For this we consider the nonlocal energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{{\lvert v(x+h)-v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\,dx

with the constant

(2.2)

\displaystyle\kappa_{d,s}=\frac{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+s)}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}{\lvert\Gamma(-s)\rvert}}.

Then the fractional Laplacian can be defined as the operator appears in the Euler-Lagrange equation, i.e., for
v,w\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s})^{\prime}(v)(w)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(-\Delta)^{s}v\,w\,dx.

The constant
\kappa_{d,s}
above is chosen such that no additional constant appears in (
2.3
). We refer to
[
Kwa17
]
for comparison of many possible equivalent definitions of
(-\Delta)^{s}
.

It is possible to compute
(-\Delta)^{s}v
by

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}v(x)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{d,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{2v(x)-v(x+h)-v(x-h)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh

\displaystyle=-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{v(x+h)-v(x)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh,

where the second integral is a principal value integral. The principal value integral is defined as the limit of the integral over
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{r}(0)
for
r\to 0
or even
B_{1/r}(0)\setminus B_{r}(0)
for
r\to 0
.
The equivalence of the two definitions can be seen by the following computation in the distributional sense

(2.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\bigg[\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{v(\cdot+h)-v(\cdot)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\bigg](\xi)&=\bigg(\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{\exp(2\pi i\xi\cdot h)-1}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\bigg)\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)\\
&=\mathcal{F}({\lvert\cdot\rvert}^{-d-2s})(\xi)\mathcal{F}[v](\xi).\end{split}

Note that for all
t\notin 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
the following is true (as a tempered distribution):

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}({\lvert\cdot\rvert}^{-d-t})(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pi^{t+\frac{d}{2}}\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{t}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{d+t}{2})}{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{t}.

Thus, we get with
t=2s

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\bigg[-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{v(\cdot+h)-v(\cdot)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\bigg](\xi)

\displaystyle=-2\kappa_{d,s}\pi^{2s+\frac{d}{2}}\frac{\Gamma(-s)}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+s)}{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{2s}\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)

\displaystyle=(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2s}\mathcal{F}[v](\xi).

This briefly verifies the equivalence of the two approaches. For the rigorous proof, see the proof of
[
DPV12
, Proposition 3.3]
.

2.2.
Fractional Sobolev spaces

We always denote by
s\in(0,1)
the fractional differentiability. For
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
being an open set with continuous boundary, we define the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,2}(\Omega)
as the set of all measurable functions
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that the following seminorm

\displaystyle{\lvert v\rvert}_{W^{s,2}(\Omega)}\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{-d-2s}{\lvert v(x)-v(y)\rvert}^{2}\,dx\,dy

is finite. We denote the norm in
W^{s,2}(\Omega)
as
{\lVert v\rVert}_{W^{s,2}(\Omega)}\coloneqq{\lVert v\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+{\lvert v\rvert}_{W^{s,2}(\Omega)}
. The other fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
is defined as

\displaystyle W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{v\in W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\,v\equiv 0\,\,\text{in}\,\,\Omega^{\complement}\right\}.

Then
W^{s,2}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
are Banach spaces, and
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{s,2}(\Omega)
holds. Moreover, we have continuous embeddings
W^{s,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
. Moreover,
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
by
[
FSV15
, Theorem 6]
.

2.3.
Constant matrix-valued coefficient

To get some inspiration for a good nonlocal model we start with the case of a constant matrix coefficient. For this let
{\mathbb{A}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{>0}}
, where
{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{>0}}
is the set of symmetric positive-definite matrices in
{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
. Let
{\mathbb{M}}\coloneqq\sqrt{{\mathbb{A}}}
be its positive-definite square root. We define

(2.6)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}u

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{A}}\nabla u)=\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{M}}^{2}\nabla u).

The fractional Laplacian can be obtained by taking the Fourier symbol
(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2}
of the Laplacian
-\Delta
to a fractional power
s\in(0,1)
. Then the new Fourier symbol
(2\pi{\lvert\xi\rvert})^{2s}
defines the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{s}
. We want to proceed similarly in order to construct
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}
, in this section for a constant matrix
{\mathbb{A}}
. The Fourier symbol of
-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}
is
(2\pi{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}\xi\rvert})^{2}
and can be calculated as follows:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}}u\big](\xi)\!=\!\mathcal{F}\big[\!-\!\operatorname{div}({\mathbb{A}}\nabla u)\big](\xi)\!=\!4\pi^{2}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}\xi},{\xi}\rangle}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)\!=\!\left(2\pi{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}\xi\rvert}\right)^{2}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi).

Hence, we define
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}u\big](\xi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 4^{s}\pi^{2s}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}\xi},{\xi}\rangle}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)=\left(2\pi{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}\xi\rvert}\right)^{2s}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi),

where the variable
\xi
of the Fourier symbol of
(-\Delta)^{s}
is replaced by
{\mathbb{M}}\xi
to obtain
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}
. Similar to (
2.4
) we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big[(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}u\big](\xi)

\displaystyle=2\kappa_{d,s}\bigg(\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{\exp(2\pi i{\mathbb{M}}\xi\cdot h)-1}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\bigg)\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)

\displaystyle=2\kappa_{d,s}\bigg(\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{\exp(2\pi i\xi\cdot{\mathbb{M}}h)-1}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\bigg)\mathcal{F}[u](\xi),

where we used the symmetry of
{\mathbb{M}}
. This and the positive definiteness of
{\mathbb{M}}
imply

(2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}u(x)&=-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{u(x+{\mathbb{M}}h)-u(x)}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\\
&=-2\kappa_{d,s}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{u(x+h)-u(x)}{{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}^{-1}h\rvert}^{d+2s}\det({\mathbb{M}})}\,dh.\end{split}

Note that
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}})^{s}u(x)=0
is the Euler-Lagrange equation of the following energy:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{J}^{s}_{\mathbb{A}}(v)&=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\!\frac{{\lvert v(x+{\mathbb{M}}h)\!-\!v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\,dx\\
&=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\!\frac{{\lvert v(x+h)\!-\!v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}^{-1}h\rvert}^{d+2s}\det({\mathbb{M}})}\,dh\,dx.\end{aligned}

In the next subsection, we introduce the coefficient as the matrix-valued function
{\mathbb{M}}(x)
on
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, instead of the constant matrix
{\mathbb{M}}
.

2.4.
Model with matrix-valued coefficient

Inspired by the nonlocal energy (
2.9
) for a constant matrix-valued coefficient, we now consider an energy for a non-constant matrix-valued coefficient. For
{\mathbb{A}}\,:\,{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{>0}}
and
{\mathbb{M}}(x)=\sqrt{{\mathbb{A}}(x)}
we define the energy

(2.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{{\mathbb{A}}(\cdot)}(v)&\coloneqq\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{{\lvert v(x+{\mathbb{M}}(x)h)-v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\,dh\,dx\\
&=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{{\lvert v(x+h)-v(x)\rvert}^{2}}{{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}^{-1}(x)h\rvert}^{d+2s}\det({\mathbb{M}}(x))}\,dh\,dx.\end{split}

Then the Euler-Lagrange equation of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{{\mathbb{A}}(\cdot)}
is given as

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\frac{\widetilde{k}(x;h)+\widetilde{k}(x+h;-h)}{2}\big(v(x+h)-v(x)\big)\,dh=0

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}(x;h)=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\frac{1}{{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}^{-1}(x)h\rvert}^{d+2s}\det({\mathbb{M}}(x))}=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\frac{1}{{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}(x)h},{h}\rangle}^{\frac{d+2s}{2}}\sqrt{\det({\mathbb{A}}(x))}}.

Remark 2.1
.

Suppose that
\lambda\mathrm{Id}\leq{\mathbb{M}}(\cdot)\leq\Lambda\mathrm{Id}
for some
0<\lambda\leq\Lambda
. Then it is possible to show that for any
v\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, the energy
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{{\mathbb{A}}(\cdot)}(v)
converges to a multiple of
\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}{\lvert{\mathbb{M}}(x)\nabla v\rvert}^{2}\,dx
for
s\to 1
. The arguments are similar to
[
Voi17
,
FKV20
]
.
Note that the energy in (
2.10
) has a positive kernel
\widetilde{k}(x;h)
for every
{\mathbb{A}}(x)>0
.

2.5.
Model using Taylor approximation

Unfortunately, the model in Section
2.4
is too difficult to analyze with respect to Meyers-type example. The nonlinear denominator makes it impossible to calculate the integral using the Fourier transform. Therefore, we use for our Meyers-type example a Taylor approximation of the energy in (
2.10
) and derive another simpler nonlocal energy with coefficients.

For this we consider the matrix coefficient
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}
from the classical Meyers’ example, see (
1.5
) as a perturbation of the identity matrix, i.e., for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{1}=\frac{x_{1}}{{\lvert x\rvert}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{x}=\frac{x}{{\lvert x\rvert}}.

With
(\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x})_{i,j}=\widehat{x}_{i}\widehat{x}_{j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq d
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)=(1-\varepsilon)\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}=\mathrm{Id}-\varepsilon\big(\mathrm{Id}-\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}\big)\eqqcolon\mathrm{Id}-\varepsilon{\mathbb{G}}.

Let us perform a Taylor approximation of the energy (
2.10
) with respect to
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Id}-\varepsilon{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}=\sum_{i\geq 0}(\varepsilon{\mathbb{G}})^{i}=1+\varepsilon{\mathbb{G}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})

and
\det{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}=\det(\mathrm{Id}-\varepsilon{\mathbb{G}})=1-\varepsilon\textrm{tr}{\mathbb{G}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})
. Hence, we can approximate
\widetilde{k}(x;h)
from Section
2.4
by the following calculation

(2.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}h},{h}\rangle}^{-\frac{d}{2}-s}(\det{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon})^{-\frac{1}{2}}$\mbox{}\hfil\qquad&\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big(1+\varepsilon{\langle{{\mathbb{G}}\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)^{-\frac{d}{2}-s}\big(1-\varepsilon\textrm{tr}{\mathbb{G}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big(1-\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon{\langle{{\mathbb{G}}\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\big(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\textrm{tr}{\mathbb{G}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\textrm{tr}{\mathbb{G}}-\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon{\langle{{\mathbb{G}}\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big(\tfrac{2-2s}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{d+2s}{2}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big(\tfrac{2-2s}{2}+\tfrac{d}{2}{\big\langle{({\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{d}(\mathrm{tr}{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{Id}\big)\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\big\rangle}+\tfrac{2s}{2}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\\
&={\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\big[{\big\langle{\big(\big(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon(1+2s)}{2}\big)\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{\varepsilon(d+2s)}{2}\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}\big)\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\big\rangle}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big].\end{aligned}

Neglecting the term
O({\lvert{\mathbb{G}}\rvert}^{2})
we obtain a new energy for our Meyers-type result.

Definition 2.2

(Meyers-type energy)
.

For
s\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)\coloneqq\dfrac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y){\lvert v(x)-v(y)\rvert}^{2}\,dx\,dy,

with
k_{s,\varepsilon}\,:\,{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to[0,\infty)
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{>0}}
given by

\displaystyle k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq|x-y|^{-d-2s}{\big\langle{\tfrac{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)}{2}\widehat{x-y}},{\widehat{x-y}}\big\rangle},

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(1-\tfrac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\big)\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}.

Then we note that

•

{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)
is a uniformly elliptic coefficient, since
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)
has eigenvalue
1+\frac{d-1}{2}\varepsilon
with eigenvector
\widehat{x}
, and eigenvalue
1-\frac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon
with eigenspace
(\mathrm{span}{\{{\widehat{x}}\}})^{\perp}
.

•

k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)
is symmetric, i.e.,
k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)=k_{s,\varepsilon}(y,x)
,

•

and
k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)\eqsim|x-y|^{-d-2s}
.

Here, we assume
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
since it ensures that
1-\frac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon>0
.

2.6.
Limit
s\to 1

We begin our analysis by showing that the limit
s\nearrow 1
recovers the energy used in the classical Meyers’ example.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
v\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, with
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)
as in (
1.5
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)\nabla v(x)},{\nabla v(x)}\rangle}\,dx.

Proof.

By the definition of kernel
k_{s,\varepsilon}
in Definition
2.2
and
[
Voi17
, Remark 4.5]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)\nabla v(x)},{\nabla v(x)}\rangle}\,dx

holds. We have to show that
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(x)
with

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}+1\big)}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma\otimes\sigma{\big\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{1,\varepsilon}(x)\sigma},{\sigma}\big\rangle}\,d\sigma.

Note that for each orthogonal matrix
Q
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(Qx)=Q\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(x)Q^{t},\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(Qx)=Q\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(x)Q^{t}.

Moreover,
\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}
and

{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}
are 0-homogeneous. Thus, it suffices to prove
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(e_{1})=\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(e_{1})
. By definition of
{\mathbb{A}}_{1,\varepsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(e_{1})\big)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}+1\big)}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}\bigg[\Big(1-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon\Big)\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\,d\sigma+\frac{d+2}{2}\varepsilon\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{1}^{2}\,d\sigma\bigg].

Here, from
[
Fol01
]
we have

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}\delta_{ij}&\quad\;\,\,\text{if $i=j$},\\
0&\quad\;\,\,\text{else},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{1}^{2}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }i\neq j,\\
\frac{3}{d+2}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}&\quad\text{if }i=j=1,\\
\frac{1}{d+2}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}&\quad\text{if }i=j\neq 1.\end{cases}

By merging the above three computations and recalling
\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\varepsilon}(e_{1})=\left[\left(1-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon\right)\mathrm{Id}+\frac{d+2}{2}\varepsilon\left(\dfrac{1}{d+2}\mathrm{Id}+\dfrac{2}{d+2}(e_{1}\otimes e_{1})\right)\right]={\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(e_{1}).

The proof is complete.
∎

2.7.
Renormalized energy

We use the same function as in the local Meyers’ example, namely
u_{\delta}(x):={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}
for some
\delta\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
. Our goal is that
u_{\delta}
is a minimizer of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}
for a suitable coupling of
\delta
and
\varepsilon
.

Unfortunately,
u_{\delta}
is not in the energy space, i.e.,
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\delta})=\infty
because of the growth of
u_{\delta}
at infinity. However, this technical problem can be solved by renormalization. For this we renormalize the energy by means of the boundary values. Since
u_{\delta}
is supposed to be the solution later (for suitable
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta,d,s)
), we use
u_{\delta}
itself here as the boundary value.
We define the renormalized energy
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dfrac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\hskip-5.69054ptk_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)\big({\lvert v(y)\!-\!v(x)\rvert}^{2}\!-\!{\lvert u_{\delta}(y)\!-\!u_{\delta}(x)\rvert}^{2}\big)\,dy\,dx.

Later we have to choose a special combination of
\delta
and
\varepsilon
depending on
s
such that
u
will indeed be a minimizer of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
we have
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(u_{\delta})=0
. In Proposition
2.6
we will show that
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
is well defined on
u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
with

\displaystyle B\coloneqq B_{1}(0).

Note that the limit
R\to\infty
is only necessary for values of
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. For
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
one could directly take integrals over
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
without a limit.

It would be possible in the renormalization to replace
u_{\delta}
by any function that agrees with
u_{\delta}
on
(2B)^{c}
and is in
W^{s,2}(4B)
. For example the function could be chosen to be smooth on
2B
.

Remark 2.4
.

The renormalized energy is defined such that it has the same first variation as the standard energy
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v)
, but allows for a larger class of boundary values and arguments. It is necessary only, since the boundary value function has infinite energy. We refer to
[
CMY17
,
DPV19
]
for further discussions.

An alternative method of renormalization is to restrict the integral over
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
to
(B^{c}\times B^{c})^{c}
, see for example
[
DK19
]
. However, the almost linear growth of
u_{\delta}
(for
\delta
small) would imply
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(u_{\delta})<\infty
which still requires
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. The renormalization in (
2.12
), however, allows to consider the full range
s\in(0,1)
.

For future reference let us recall the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
be given. For any
z,w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|z-w|\leq\frac{1}{2}|w|
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\dfrac{z}{|z|^{\alpha}}-\dfrac{w}{|w|^{\alpha}}\right|\leq c(\alpha)\dfrac{|z-w|}{|w|^{\alpha}}.

Using this, we prove basic properties of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
s\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. The functional
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
is well-defined and finite on
u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. It is coercive and uniformly convex on
u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
in the sense that for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
we have

(2.13)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v_{1})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v_{2})-\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\Big(\frac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2}\Big)\eqsim{\lvert v_{1}-v_{2}\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}

with the implicit constant
c=c(d,s)
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
is well-defined and bounded from below. Let
v=u_{\delta}+w
with
w\in W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. Since
s-\frac{d}{2}<0<1-\delta
we have
u_{\delta}\in W^{s,2}(2B)
. Moreover,
{\lVert u_{\delta}\rVert}_{W^{s,2}(2B)}
is bounded uniformly for
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Using
v=u_{\delta}+w
,

\displaystyle{\lvert v(x)\!-\!v(y)\rvert}^{2}\!-\!{\lvert u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\rvert}^{2}

\displaystyle={\lvert w(x)\!-\!w(y)\rvert}^{2}\!-\!2\big(w(x)\!-\!w(y)\big)\big(u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\big)

is obtained. This proves that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(w)-\kappa_{d,s}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\!\!k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)\big(w(x)\!-\!w(y)\big)\big(u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\big)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(w)-\mathrm{I}.

We will below show that
\mathrm{I}
is well defined and

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\lesssim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\quad\text{ uniformly for $\delta\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]$.}

This then implies that
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(w)
is well defined. Moreover,
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon}(w)\gtrsim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}
also implies the coercivity of
w\mapsto\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(u_{\delta}+w)
with respect to
{\lVert w\rVert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}^{2}
.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{2B}\int_{2B}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y){\big\lvert w(x)\!-\!w(y)\big\rvert}\,{\big\lvert u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\big\rvert}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}(2B)}{\lvert u_{\delta}\rvert}_{W^{s,2}(2B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}

with the implicit constant
c=c(d,s,\delta)
.
Define
A_{R}\coloneqq(B_{R}(0)\times B_{R}(0))\setminus(2B\times 2B)
. By symmetry, we now consider

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}\coloneqq\lim_{R\to\infty}\iint_{A_{R}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)w(x)\big(u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\big)\,dy\,dx.

Using
w=0
on
B^{c}
and symmetry we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=2\lim_{R\to\infty}\,\int_{B}\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)w(x)\big(u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(y)\big)\,dy\,dx.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{B}\int_{(2B)^{c}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y){\lvert w(x)\rvert}{\lvert u_{\delta}(x)\rvert}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lVert w\rVert}_{L^{2}(B)}{\lVert u_{\delta}\rVert}_{L^{2}(B)}\lesssim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}.

It remains to consider

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-2\lim_{R\to\infty}\,\int_{B}\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)w(x)u_{\delta}(y)\,dy\,dx.

If
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, then
{\lvert u_{\delta}(y)\rvert}\leq{\lvert y\rvert}^{1-\delta}\leq{\lvert y\rvert}
on
(2B)^{c}
would allow us to control
\mathrm{III}
similar to the calculations above. However, for the full range
s\in(0,1)
we need some cancellation property.
Recall that

\displaystyle k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)

\displaystyle={\big\langle{\tfrac{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)}{2}\tfrac{x-y}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}},{\tfrac{x-y}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}}\big\rangle}.

Let us define

\displaystyle g(x,y)

\displaystyle={\big\langle{\tfrac{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)}{2}\tfrac{y}{{\lvert y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}},{\tfrac{y}{{\lvert y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}}\big\rangle}.

Since
u_{\delta}
is odd,
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)
and
y\mapsto g(x,y)
is even, and the set
B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B=B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B_{1}(0)
is symmetric, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B}\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B}g(x,y){\big\langle{\tfrac{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(y)}{2}\widehat{y}},{\widehat{y}}\big\rangle}w(x)u_{\delta}(y)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle=-2\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B}\int_{B_{R}(0)\setminus 2B}\big(k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)-g(x,y)\big)w(x)u_{\delta}(y)\,dy\,dx.

It follows from
{\lvert{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}\rvert}\lesssim 1
and Lemma
2.5
that for
x\in B
and
y\in(2B)^{c}
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\lvert k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)-g(x,y)\rvert}&\lesssim{\Big\lvert\tfrac{x-y}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}-\tfrac{y}{{\lvert y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}\Big\rvert}{\Big\lvert\tfrac{x-y}{{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}+\tfrac{y}{{\lvert y\rvert}^{d/2+s+1}}\Big\rvert}\\
&\lesssim{\lvert x\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert}^{-d-2s-1}.\end{split}

This implies that

\displaystyle\int_{B}\int_{(2B)^{c}}{\big\lvert k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)-g(x,y)\big\rvert}{\lvert w(x)\rvert}{\lvert u_{\delta}(y)\rvert}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B}\int_{(2B)^{c}}{\lvert x\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert}^{-d-2s-1}{\lvert w(x)\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert}^{1-\delta}\,dy\,dx\lesssim{\lVert w\rVert}_{L^{1}(B)}\lesssim{\lvert w\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}.

Overall, this shows that
\mathrm{III}
,
\mathrm{II}
and
\mathrm{I}
are well defined and that (
2.14
) holds.

A straightforward calculation shows that for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v_{1})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v_{2})-\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\Big(\frac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y){\lvert(v_{1}-v_{2})(y)-(v_{1}-v_{2})(x)\rvert}^{2}\,dy\,dx\eqsim{\lvert v_{1}-v_{2}\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}.

This proves the uniform convexity in the sense of (
2.13
).
∎

2.8.
Euler–Langrange equation

Proposition
2.6
ensures that we can apply the standard theory of variational integrals to
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
, which ensures the existence of a unique minimizer of

(2.16)

\displaystyle\bar{u}_{\varepsilon,\delta}\coloneqq\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_{v\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)}\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v).

We remark that later in our nonlocal Meyers’ example
\delta
and
\varepsilon
are chosen such that
u_{\delta}
itself is the solution, i.e.,
u_{\delta}=\bar{u}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
. Let us now derive the Euler-Lagrange equation. For this we need the derivative of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
. Let
v\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
and
\eta\in W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. Then for
\tau>0
we calculate the difference quotient

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v+\tau\eta)-\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(v)}{\tau}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\kappa_{d,s}}{2}\lim_{R\to\infty}\hskip-2.84526pt\iint_{B_{R}(0)\times B_{R}(0)}\hskip-8.53581pt\!\!\!\!\!k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)\big(2(v(x)\!-\!v(y))(\eta(x)\!-\!\eta(y))+\tau{\lvert\eta(y)\!-\!\eta(x)\rvert}^{2}\big)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle=\kappa_{d,s}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\!\!k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(v(x)-v(y))(\eta(x)-\eta(y))\,dy\,dx+\tau\mathcal{J}^{s}(\eta).

This proves that the Gateaux derivative of
\mathcal{J}_{D}^{s}
exists and

(2.17)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(v)(\eta)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{d,s}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\!\!\!k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(v(x)\!-\!v(y))(\eta(x)\!-\!\eta(y))\,dy\,dx

for all
v\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
and
\eta\in W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
.

Similar to the calculation in Proposition
2.6
one can show that

(2.18)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(v)(\eta)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+{\lvert v-u_{\delta}\rvert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}\big){\lVert\eta\rVert}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})}

uniformly for
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Thus, the Gateaux derivative is linear and bounded on
W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. The density of
C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
to
W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
allows us to restrict ourselves sometimes to
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
.
Overall, we have derived the Euler-Lagrange equation:

(2.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon,\delta})(\eta)&=0\end{split}

for all
\eta\in W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. Moreover,
v\in u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
is the minimizer of
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
over
u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
if and only if
(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(v)(\eta)=0
for all
\eta\in W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
.

3.
Strong formulation

Later for our Meyers-type example we want to find
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta,s)
such that
u_{\delta}=\bar{u}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
, i.e. that
u_{\delta}
minimizes
\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
over
u_{\delta}+W^{s,2}_{0}(B)
. For this we have to check that
(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)=0
for all
\eta\in W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
. Due to the boundedness of
(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})
(see (
2.18
)) and the density of
C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
in
W^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
it suffices to verify that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle=0

for all
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
for suitable
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta,d,s)
.

In the following we will derive a version of
(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)
. In particular, we will introduce an object
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}(u_{\delta})
such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle={\langle{(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}},{\eta}\rangle}

for all
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
.
We start by introducing
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Then for all
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus{\{{0}\}}
the limit

\displaystyle((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)\coloneqq 2\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh

exists. Moreover,
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}\in L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
and all
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle{\big\lvert((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)\big\rvert}\!\leq\!2\sup_{R\geq 1}{\bigg\lvert\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}\hskip-11.38109ptk_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x\!+\!h)(u_{\delta}(x)\!-\!u_{\delta}(x\!+\!h))dh\bigg\rvert}\!\lesssim\!{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta-2s}.

Proof.

Let
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus{\{{0}\}}
and
R\geq 2
. We define

\displaystyle U_{R}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh.

Now, let
R>r\geq 2
. Then

\displaystyle U_{R}(x)-U_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\frac{1}{r}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{r<{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)u_{\delta}(x)\,dh

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{r<{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh\coloneqq\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III}.

Note that
\mathrm{II}\lesssim r^{-2s}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}
.
For
\mathrm{III}
, since
u_{\delta}(h)
is odd and
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(h)
is even, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{r<{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}\frac{{\langle{({\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)+{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(h))\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}(-u_{\delta}(h))\,dh=0.

On the other hand with
{\lvert x\rvert}\leq 1
, using (
2.15
) and
{\lvert u_{\delta}(h)-u_{\delta}(x+h)\rvert}\lesssim{\lvert h\rvert}^{-\delta}{\lvert x\rvert}
,

\displaystyle{\big\lvert\mathrm{III}-\mathrm{III}_{2}\big\rvert}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{r<{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}{\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\frac{{\lvert x\rvert}}{{\lvert h\rvert}}\big(1+{\lvert h\rvert}^{1-\delta}\big)\,dh\lesssim r^{-2s}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}

is obtained. We turn to
\mathrm{I}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{2}\coloneqq\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\min{\{{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert},\frac{1}{r}}\}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}<{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh.

Then

\displaystyle{\big\lvert\mathrm{I}-\mathrm{I}_{2}\big\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\frac{1}{r}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h){\lvert u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h)\rvert}\,dh

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\frac{1}{r}\geq\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}\int_{{\lvert x\rvert}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}}{\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\,dh\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\frac{1}{r}\geq\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta-2s}.

For
{\lvert h\rvert}\leq\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}
we have

\displaystyle{\lvert u_{\delta}(x+h)-u_{\delta}(x)-\nabla u_{\delta}(x)h\rvert}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lvert x\rvert}^{-1-\delta}{\lvert h\rvert}^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{3}\coloneqq\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\min{\{{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert},\frac{1}{r}}\}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}<{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)\nabla u_{\delta}(x)h\,dh.

Then

\displaystyle{\lvert\mathrm{I}_{2}-\mathrm{I}_{3}\rvert}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\min{\{{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert},\frac{1}{r}}\}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}<{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}{\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s+2}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-1-\delta}\,dh

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\min{\{{{\lvert x\rvert},\tfrac{1}{r}}\}}\big)^{2(1-s)}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-1-\delta}.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{4}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\min{\{{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert},\frac{1}{r}}\}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}<{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}\frac{2{\langle{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)\widehat{h}},{\widehat{h}}\rangle}}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\nabla u_{\delta}(x)h\,dh=0,

since the integrand is odd with respect to
h
. Thus,

\displaystyle{\lvert\mathrm{I}_{3}\rvert}

\displaystyle={\lvert\mathrm{I}_{3}-\mathrm{I}_{4}\rvert}\lesssim\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}<\min{\{{\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert},\frac{1}{r}}\}}}{\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}\frac{{\lvert h\rvert}}{{\lvert x\rvert}}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-\delta}{\lvert h\rvert}\,dh

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\min{\{{{\lvert x\rvert},\tfrac{1}{r}}\}}\big)^{2(1-s)}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-1-\delta}.

Overall, we have for
{\lvert x\rvert}\leq 1
and
R\geq r\geq 1
that

\displaystyle{\lvert U_{R}(x)-U_{r}(x)\rvert}

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{-2s}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\frac{1}{r}\geq\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}}\}}{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta-2s}+\big(\min{\{{{\lvert x\rvert},\tfrac{1}{r}}\}}\big)^{2(1-s)}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-1-\delta}.

This proves that for every
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus{\{{0}\}}
,
U_{r}(x)
is a Cauchy sequence for
r\to\infty
. Thus
((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)=\lim_{r\to\infty}U_{r}(x)
exists for all
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus\{0\}
. Moreover, for each
x\in B_{1}(0)\setminus{\{{0}\}}
we estimate

\displaystyle{\lvert((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq{\lvert U_{1}(x)\rvert}+\sup_{r\geq 1}{\lvert U_{R}(x)-U_{2}(x)\rvert}\lesssim 0+{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta-2s}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

The next step is to verify (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\delta,\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Then for all
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)\eta(x)\,dx.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
. Then by (
2.17
) we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{d,s}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\!\!\!k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(y))(\eta(x)-\eta(y))\,dy\,dx.

In the following let
R\geq 2
. We define

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(y))(\eta(x)-\eta(y))\,dy\,dx,

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert x-y\rvert}\geq\frac{1}{R}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(y))(\eta(x)-\eta(y))\,dy\,dx.

Then since
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, by using
{\lvert{\lvert a\rvert}^{-\delta}a-{\lvert b\rvert}^{-\delta}b\rvert}\lesssim({\lvert a\rvert}+{\lvert b\rvert})^{-\delta}{\lvert a-b\rvert}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle{\lvert\mathrm{I}-\mathrm{II}\rvert}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert}_{\infty}\int_{B_{2}(0)}\int_{B_{2}(0)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert x-y\rvert}<\frac{1}{R}}\}}{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{-d-2s+2}\big({\lvert x\rvert}+{\lvert y\rvert}\big)^{-\delta}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim{\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert}_{\infty}\,R^{2s-2}

holds. This proves that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathrm{I}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathrm{II}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=2\int_{B_{R}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert x-y\rvert}\geq\frac{1}{R}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,y)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(y))\,dy\,\eta(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{B_{1}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(-x)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}\geq\frac{1}{R}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh\,\eta(x)\,dx.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\int_{B_{1}(0)}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{{\lvert h\rvert}\geq\frac{1}{R}}\}}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh\,\eta(x)\,dx.

Then for
R\geq 2

\displaystyle{\lvert\mathrm{II}-\mathrm{III}\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{1}(0)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{R-1\leq{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R+1}\}}{\lvert h\rvert}^{-d-2s}{\lvert u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h)\rvert}\,dh\,\eta(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{d-1}R^{-d-2s}R^{1-\delta}\lesssim R^{-2s}.

Overall, we get that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathrm{I}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle\quad\quad=2\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{1}(0)}\int_{\frac{1}{R}\leq{\lvert h\rvert}\leq R}k_{s,\varepsilon}(x,x+h)(u_{\delta}(x)-u_{\delta}(x+h))\,dh\,\eta(x)\,dx.

Now, by Lemma
3.1
and the theorem of dominated convergence, we conclude

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{s}_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{\prime}(u_{\delta})(\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{1}(0)}((-\Delta_{\mathbb{A}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta})(x)\eta(x)\,dx.

This proves the claim.
∎

4.
Nonlocal Meyers’ example

We are now ready to present our nonlocal Meyers’ example in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
d\geq 2
and to prove our main result mentioned in the introduction. Recall that for
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
we defined in (
1.9
) that

(4.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)=\big(1-\tfrac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\big)\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x},\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}.

The main goal of this section is to show the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1

(Nonlocal Meyers’ example)
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and
s\in(0,1)
.

(a)

With
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, the function
u_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle u_{\delta}\in W_{{\mathrm{loc}}}^{t,q}(B_{1}(0))\quad\text{if and only if}\quad 1-\delta>t-\frac{d}{q},

where
t\in(0,1)
and
q\in(1,\infty)
.

(b)

With
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, the matrix-valued coefficient
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)=\left(1-\frac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\right)\mathrm{Id}+\frac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x},

has the following properties:

(i)

It is uniformly elliptic with eigenvalues in the range

\displaystyle\big[1-\tfrac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon,1+\tfrac{d-1}{2}\varepsilon\big]\subset\big[\tfrac{1}{4},1+\tfrac{d-1}{4}\big].

(ii)

It satisfies the smallness assumption

(4.2)

\displaystyle{\lVert\log{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\tfrac{1+d+4s}{2}\varepsilon,

where the matrix is measured in the spectral norm.

(c)

There exists
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(d,s)\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
such that the following holds: For any
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, there exists
\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}]
with
\varepsilon\leq C(d,s)\delta
such that
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)=0
as a function on
B_{1}(0)
. The relation of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
is given in (
4.16
).

The proof of this theorem is given at the end of this section.
Also, recalling
{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}(x)=(1-\varepsilon)\mathrm{Id}+\varepsilon\widehat{x}\otimes\widehat{x}
,
{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}(x)
and
u_{\delta}(x)
as in (
4.1
), we will prove the following.

Theorem 4.2

(Robustness as
s\nearrow 1
)
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and
s\in(0,1)
. We have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow 1}(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)=(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{\varepsilon}})u_{\delta}(x)

for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, any
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

We postpone the proof of this theorem to the end of this section.

Remark 4.3

(Vectorial case)
.

Meyers’ example becomes more elegant, when using the vector-valued function
u_{\delta}(x)={\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}
. Our approach works similarly for the vectorial case. The restriction to the first component
\widehat{x}_{1}
is only to emphasize that the scalar case is already critical. In this linear context the vectorial case just decouples into several scalar problems.

The case
d=2
in Theorem
4.1
of special interest, since it provides the example with the worst regularity. Indeed, the homogeneity of our solution
u_{\delta}
is
1-\delta\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
. However, the index of
W^{s,2}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}
is
s-\frac{d}{2}\in(-\frac{d}{2},1-\frac{d}{2})
decreases fast in
d
. Therefore, the gap of additional regularity becomes larger for
d
increasing. This makes the case
d=2
the most important one. Therefore, we state in the following some simplifications for the case
d=2
.

Proposition 4.4

(Simplification for
d=2
)
.

Suppose that
d=2
. Then we have
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)=0
as a function on
B_{1}(0)
, whenever

(4.3)

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{s(2-s)-\frac{\Gamma(s+1)\Gamma\left(3-s-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\Gamma(1+\frac{\delta}{2})}{\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(2-\frac{\delta}{2})\Gamma(s+\frac{\delta}{2})}}.

Moreover,
for any
s\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{2s^{2}}{1-s})
, we have

(4.4)

\displaystyle\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{s(2-s)}\leq\varepsilon\leq\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{(2-s)(s-(1-s)(s+\frac{\delta}{2}))}.

Note that
\varepsilon\to(2-\delta)\delta
as
s\nearrow 1
in agreement with (
1.6
).

Proof.

The formula (
4.3
) is a special case of Corollary
4.7
, which we prove below.
We can write (
4.3
) as

(4.5)

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{s(2-s)-(1-s)(2-s)\left(2-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\underbrace{\frac{\Gamma(s+1)\Gamma\left(3-s-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\Gamma(1+\frac{\delta}{2})}{\Gamma(3-s)\Gamma(3-\frac{\delta}{2})\Gamma(s+1+\frac{\delta}{2})}}_{=:G(s,\delta)}}.

The first inequality in (
4.4
) is clear since
G(s,\delta)>0
. We claim that
G(s,\delta)\leq\frac{1}{2}
. For this we need
[
Alz97
, Theorem 10]
, where it has been shown that
0\leq a_{1}\leq\dots a_{m}
and
0\leq b_{1}\leq\dots b_{m}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}
for
k=1,\dots,m
implies

(4.6)

\displaystyle x\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\Gamma(x+a_{i})}{\Gamma(x+b_{i})}.

is decreasing on
(0,\infty)
. Applying this to
a_{1}=1
,
a_{2}=3-\frac{\delta}{2}
,
b_{1}=3-s-\frac{\delta}{2}
,
b_{2}=1+s
and
x=\frac{\delta}{2}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(1)\Gamma(3-\frac{\delta}{2})}{\Gamma(3-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\Gamma(1+s)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Gamma(1+\frac{\delta}{2})\Gamma(3)}{\Gamma(3-s)\Gamma(1+s+\frac{\delta}{2})}.

This implies that
G(s,\delta)\leq\frac{\Gamma(1)}{\Gamma(3)}=\frac{1}{2}
as desired. Therefore, for (
4.5
) we estimate

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{s(2-s)-\frac{1}{2}(1-s)(2-s)\left(2-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{s(2-s)-(1-s)(2-s)\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}=\dfrac{2(2-s-\frac{\delta}{2})\delta}{(2-s)(s-(1-s)(s+\frac{\delta}{2}))},

provided
s>(1\!-\!s)(s\!+\!\frac{\delta}{2})
which is equivalent to
\delta<\frac{2s^{2}}{1-s}
. This condition ensures that the denominator does not change its sign. This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 4.5

(Rotation and scaling properties for
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u
)
.

We display the following properties of
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(y)
.

•

Rotation property: For any
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
QQ^{T}=\mathrm{Id}
, we have

\displaystyle(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(Qy)=(Q(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}U_{\delta}(y))_{1},

where
U_{\delta}(y):={\lvert y\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{y}
.

•

Scaling property: For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(\lambda y)=\operatorname{sgn}{\lambda}|\lambda|^{1-2s-\delta}(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(y)
.

Thus, we see that
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)=0
is equivalent to
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})=0
.

To show Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
, we first compute
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})
and then use Remark
4.5
to recover
(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(x)
. We write

(4.7)

\displaystyle(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{A}}_{s,\varepsilon}})^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})=-2\kappa_{d,s}\left[\left(1-\frac{1+2s}{2}\varepsilon\right)f_{1}(d,s,\delta)+\dfrac{d+2s}{2}\varepsilon f_{2}(d,s,\delta)\right],

where

(4.8)

\displaystyle f_{1}(d,s,\delta)\coloneqq\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{|e_{1}+h|^{1-\delta}\widehat{1+h_{1}}-1}{|h|^{d+2s}}\,dh=\dfrac{1}{2\kappa_{d,s}}(-\Delta)^{s}u_{\delta}(e_{1})

and

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{2}(d,s,\delta)&\coloneqq\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-2.84526pt\left<\dfrac{\widehat{e_{1}\!+\!h}\otimes\widehat{e_{1}\!+\!h}\!+\!e_{1}\otimes e_{1}}{2}\widehat{h},\widehat{h}\right>\dfrac{|e_{1}\!+\!h|^{1-\delta}\widehat{1\!+\!h_{1}}\!-\!1}{|h|^{d+2s}}dh\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\dfrac{{\langle{e_{1}+h},{h}\rangle}^{2}}{|e_{1}+h|^{2}|h|^{2}}+\dfrac{{\langle{e_{1}},{h}\rangle}^{2}}{|h|^{2}}\right)\dfrac{|e_{1}+h|^{1-\delta}\widehat{1+h_{1}}-1}{|h|^{d+2s}}\,dh.\end{split}

Now, we need to compute
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
using the Fourier transform of distributions.

Lemma 4.6

(Computation of
f_{1}(d,s,\delta)
and
f_{2}(d,s,\delta)
)
.

For
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we have

(4.10)

\displaystyle f_{1}(d,s,\delta)=-\frac{2^{2s-1}}{\kappa_{d,s}}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{d}{2}-s\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}\dfrac{\delta}{2}.

On the other hand, we have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{2}(d,s,\delta)&=\dfrac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\dfrac{\Gamma(-s+1)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+s\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-s+1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}f_{2,1}(d,s,\delta)\\
&\quad-\dfrac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\dfrac{\Gamma(-s+1)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+s\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(s\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-s\right)}\dfrac{d-1}{d+2s},\end{split}

where

(4.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f_{2,1}(d,s,\delta)\\
&=-\dfrac{2(1-s)(2s+\delta-3)(2s+\delta-1)}{(d+2s)(d-2s+2-\delta)}+\dfrac{(1-s)(2s+\delta-3)}{d-2s+2-\delta}\\
&\quad+\dfrac{(2s+\delta-1)(2s+\delta)}{d+2s}-\dfrac{(d+2s+3)(2s+\delta-1)}{d+2s}\\
&\quad+\dfrac{d+2s-1}{2}-\dfrac{(d-2s-\delta)(2s+\delta)}{2s(d+2s)}\\
&=\dfrac{(d-\delta)\left[(2s+1)\left(\delta-\frac{d-2s+2}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(d-2s+2)(2ds-d+4s^{2}-6s-2)\right]}{2s(d+2s)(d-2s+2-\delta)}.\end{split}

Proof.

First, we explain the strategy. For
f_{2}
, let us rewrite
f_{2}
in (
B.2
) as

(4.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f_{2}(d,s,\delta)\\
&=\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{-2h_{1}^{3}\!+\!|h|^{2}h_{1}^{2}\!+\!2h_{1}^{2}\!-\!2|h|^{2}h_{1}\!+\!|h|^{4}}{2|h|^{d+2s+2}|e_{1}-h|^{2}}\left(|e_{1}\!-\!h|^{-\delta}(1\!-\!h_{1})\!-\!1\right)dh,\end{split}

which formally corresponds to
(g_{3}\ast g_{4})(e_{1})
, where

\displaystyle g_{3}(h):=|h|^{-\delta-2}h_{1}\!-\!|h|^{-2}\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,g_{4}(h):=\dfrac{1}{{\lvert h\rvert}^{d+2s}}\left(\dfrac{-h_{1}^{3}}{|h|^{2}}\!+\!\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{2}\!+\!\dfrac{h_{1}^{2}}{|h|^{2}}\!-\!h_{1}\!+\!\frac{{\lvert h\rvert}^{2}}{2}\right).

In order to find an explicit formula for
g_{3}\ast g_{4}
we will use the Fourier transform and establish
\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}\ast g_{4}]\right]=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]\right]
and

\displaystyle f_{2}(d,s,\delta)=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}\ast g_{4}]\right](-e_{1})=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]\right](-e_{1})\,.

The second equality will follow from

\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}\ast g_{4}]\right](x)=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]\right](x)
. The evaluation
x=e_{1}
for
\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]\right]
then yields the desired expression for
f_{2}(d,s,\delta)
.

Let us provide details for all necessary steps in the proof.

(1)

We define
g_{3}
and
g_{4}
in the distributional sense as in Definition
A.1
. Note that
g_{3}
and
g_{4}
do not belong to
L^{1}_{{\mathrm{loc}}}
in general.

(2)

We will show that the convolution
g_{3}\ast g_{4}
is well-defined in the tempered distributional sense as in
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
. We provide the details in Lemma
B.10
and Lemma
B.11
. Note that Appendix
B
and
[
Vla02
]
denote such a convolution with the symbol
\divideontimes
.

(3)

In Lemma
B.12
we show that
f_{2}(d,s,\delta)=\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow 0}{\langle{g_{3}\ast g_{4}},{\varphi_{\sigma}}\rangle}
, where
\varphi_{\sigma}
is an approximation to the identity at
e_{1}
and defined as follows: for the standard mollifier
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
,
\varphi_{\sigma}(x):=\frac{1}{\sigma^{d}}\varphi\left(\frac{x-e_{1}}{\sigma}\right)
.

(4)

By (2), the relation
\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}\ast g_{4}]\right]=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]\right]
is available (see
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
), where the definition of product of distributions ‘
\cdot
’ is from
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
. The precise computation of
\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]
and
\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]
is given in Lemma
B.13
.

(5)

Lemma
B.14
shows that
\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]=\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]
in the distributional sense, where
\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]
means the usual multiplication of two locally integrable functions
\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]
and
\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]
.

(6)

We compute
g_{3}\ast g_{4}
via Fourier transform (see Lemma
A.3
with Theorem
B.15
). Also, in the proof of Lemma
A.3
, we observe that
\mathcal{F}[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]]
coincides with a locally integrable function near
-e_{1}
.

Overall, with
\overline{\varphi}_{\sigma}(x)=\varphi_{\sigma}(-x)
, we have

(4.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{2}(d,s,\delta)&\underset{\text{(3)}}{=}\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow 0}{\langle{g_{3}\ast g_{4}},{\varphi_{\sigma}}\rangle}\\
&\underset{\text{(4)}}{=}\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow 0}{\langle{\mathcal{F}[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]]},{\overline{\varphi}_{\sigma}}\rangle}\\
&\underset{\text{(5)}}{=}\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow 0}{\langle{\mathcal{F}[\mathcal{F}[g_{3}]\mathcal{F}[g_{4}]]},{\overline{\varphi}_{\sigma}}\rangle}\\
&\underset{\text{(6)}}{=}\dfrac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\dfrac{\Gamma(-s+1)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+s\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-s+1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}f_{2,1}(d,s,\delta)\\
&\quad-\dfrac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\dfrac{\Gamma(-s+1)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+s\right)}\dfrac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(s\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-s\right)}\dfrac{d-1}{d+2s}.\end{split}

This completes the proof of (
4.11
).

The proof in case of
f_{1}(d,s,\delta)
is similar. For some suitable distributions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we argue as follows.
In detail, when
\delta=0
, then one can see that
f_{1}(d,s,\delta)=0
. Thus we assume
\delta>0
.

(1)

We define
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in the distributional sense as follows: for
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle{\langle{g_{1}},{\varphi}\rangle}:={\langle{{\lvert x\rvert}^{1-\delta}\widehat{x}_{1}},{\varphi}\rangle}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\lvert x\rvert}^{-\delta}x_{1}\varphi(x)\,dx,

and

\displaystyle{\langle{g_{2}},{\varphi}\rangle}:={\langle{{\lvert x\rvert}^{-d-2s}},{\varphi}\rangle}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{{\lvert x\rvert}^{-d-2s+2}\Delta\varphi(x)}{2s(d+2s-2)}\,dx.

One can check the second identity by Gauss-Green formula and
2-2s>0
.

(2)

We will show that
g_{1}\ast g_{2}
is well-defined in the tempered distributional sense of
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
(See Lemma
B.2
and Lemma
B.3
. Note that the convolution is denoted by
\divideontimes
in there).

(3)

Moreover, in Lemma
B.4
we show
f_{1}(d,s,\delta)=\lim_{\sigma\rightarrow 0}{\langle{g_{1}\ast g_{2}},{\varphi_{\sigma}}\rangle}
.

(4)

By (2), the relation
\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{1}\ast g_{2}]\right]=\mathcal{F}\left[\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]\right]
is available (see
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
), where the definition of product of distributions ‘
\cdot
’ is from
[
Vla02
, Section 6.5]
. The computation of
\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]
and
\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]
is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[g_{1}](\xi)=\dfrac{1}{2-\delta}\mathcal{F}[\partial_{1}{\lvert x\rvert}^{2-\delta}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2\pi i\xi_{1}}{2-\delta}\mathcal{F}[{\lvert x\rvert}^{2-\delta}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2\pi i\xi_{1}}{2-\delta}\dfrac{(2\pi)^{\delta-2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\delta}{2}+1\right)}{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}2^{\delta-2}\Gamma\left(\frac{\delta}{2}-1\right)}{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{-d+\delta-2}

\displaystyle=-\pi^{-\frac{d}{2}+\delta-1}i\dfrac{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)}{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{-d+\delta-2}\xi_{1}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[g_{2}](\xi)=\pi^{\frac{d}{2}+2s}\dfrac{\Gamma(-s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+s\right)}{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{2s}.

(5)

Furthermore, Lemma
B.6
implies that
\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]\cdot\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]=\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]
in the distributional sense, where
\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]
means the usual multiplication of functions
\mathcal{F}[g_{1}]
and
\mathcal{F}[g_{2}]
.

(6)

We compute
g_{1}\ast g_{2}
via Fourier transform as follows:
